fully worked solution and a TikZ graph showing the two equations and their point(s) of intersection --- the
geometric meaning of the solution. Lines, parabolas, circles (drawn to scale) and hyperbolas are plotted, with
tangency, parallel and coincident cases shown where relevant.

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 01\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-001)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 01 (ise5-001)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = x + 1 \) and \( y = x^2 - 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Label the equations and choose a strategy.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\textcircled{1}\; y = x + 1 \qquad \textcircled{2}\; y = x^2 - 1
\end{gathered}\]
Both equations are already in the form "\( y = \) something". Setting the two right-hand sides equal removes \( y \) and gives a single quadratic in \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: Equate the two expressions for \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x + 1 = x^2 - 1
\end{gathered}\]
Because \( y \) equals each right-hand side, those right-hand sides must equal each other at any point of intersection.

\textit{Step 3: Rearrange into the standard form \( ax^2 + bx + c = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - x - 2 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Move every term to one side so the quadratic equals zero, which is the form needed for factorising.

\textit{Step 4: Factorise and solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 2)(x + 1) = 0 \implies x = 2 \text{ or } x = -1
\end{gathered}\]
Two distinct roots tell you the line cuts the curve in two points, so you expect two coordinate pairs.

\textit{Step 5: Substitute each \( x \) into the LINEAR equation \( \textcircled{1} \) to find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2 \Rightarrow y = 3; \qquad x = -1 \Rightarrow y = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Always substitute back into the linear equation --- it cannot introduce extra (extraneous) solutions, whereas a quadratic substitution can.

\textit{Step 6: State the solution as coordinate pairs.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x,\, y) = (2,\, 3) \text{ and } (-1,\, 0)
\end{gathered}\]
The final answer must be pairs, not just \( x \)-values. Each \( x \) is matched with its own \( y \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (2, 3)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-1, 0)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 02\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-002)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 02 (ise5-002)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = 2x + 3 \) and \( y = x^2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the two expressions for \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 2x + 3
\end{gathered}\]
Since both equations are solved for \( y \), the right-hand sides must agree wherever the graphs meet.

\textit{Step 2: Bring all terms to one side.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x - 3 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
You want the quadratic in the standard form \( ax^2 + bx + c = 0 \) before factorising.

\textit{Step 3: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 3)(x + 1) = 0 \implies x = 3 \text{ or } x = -1
\end{gathered}\]
Look for two numbers that multiply to \( -3 \) and add to \( -2 \): \( -3 \) and \( +1 \).

\textit{Step 4: Find each \( y \) using the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 3 \Rightarrow y = 2(3) + 3 = 9; \qquad x = -1 \Rightarrow y = 2(-1) + 3 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Use the linear equation to avoid the risk of generating spurious solutions.

\textit{Step 5: Verify in the quadratic equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3^2 = 9 \checkmark; \qquad (-1)^2 = 1 \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
Both pairs satisfy \( y = x^2 \), so they are genuine intersection points.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (3, 9)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-1, 1)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 03\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-003)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 03 (ise5-003)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = x - 3 \) and \( y = x^2 - 5x + 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the right-hand sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x - 3 = x^2 - 5x + 3
\end{gathered}\]
Eliminate \( y \) by setting the two expressions for \( y \) equal.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange to standard quadratic form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
0 = x^2 - 6x + 6
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract \( x - 3 \) from both sides: \( -5x - x = -6x \) and \( 3 - (-3) = 6 \).

\textit{Step 3: Use the quadratic formula because this does not factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \dfrac{6 \pm \sqrt{36 - 24}}{2} = \dfrac{6 \pm \sqrt{12}}{2} = 3 \pm \sqrt{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Simplify \( \sqrt{12} = 2\sqrt{3} \), then divide numerator and denominator by 2.

\textit{Step 4: Substitute each \( x \) into \( y = x - 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 3 + \sqrt{3} \Rightarrow y = \sqrt{3}; \qquad x = 3 - \sqrt{3} \Rightarrow y = -\sqrt{3}
\end{gathered}\]
The constants cancel cleanly because the line passes through \( (3, 0) \).

\textit{Step 5: State the two intersection points.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(3 + \sqrt{3},\, \sqrt{3}) \text{ and } (3 - \sqrt{3},\, -\sqrt{3})
\end{gathered}\]
Irrational answers are fine --- leave them as exact surds rather than rounding.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (3 + \sqrt{3}, \sqrt{3})\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (3 - \sqrt{3}, -\sqrt{3})\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 04\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-004)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 04 (ise5-004)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = 3x - 2 \) and \( y = x^2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the two expressions for \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 3x - 2
\end{gathered}\]
Both right-hand sides equal \( y \), so they must equal each other at the intersection.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 3x + 2 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Standard form with \( a = 1 \), \( b = -3 \), \( c = 2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 1)(x - 2) = 0 \implies x = 1 \text{ or } x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers multiplying to \( 2 \) and summing to \( -3 \): \( -1 \) and \( -2 \).

\textit{Step 4: Find each corresponding \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 1 \Rightarrow y = 1; \qquad x = 2 \Rightarrow y = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Use \( y = 3x - 2 \): for \( x = 1 \), \( y = 1 \); for \( x = 2 \), \( y = 4 \). Quickly check: \( 1^2 = 1 \) and \( 2^2 = 4 \) --- both consistent with the quadratic.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (1, 1)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (2, 4)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 05\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-005)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 05 (ise5-005)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = 4x \) and \( y = x^2 + 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the two expressions for \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 3 = 4x
\end{gathered}\]
Setting the right-hand sides equal eliminates \( y \) and gives a single quadratic in \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 4x + 3 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract \( 4x \) from both sides.

\textit{Step 3: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 1)(x - 3) = 0 \implies x = 1 \text{ or } x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
A standard "find two numbers that multiply to 3, add to \( -4 \)" factorisation: \( -1, -3 \).

\textit{Step 4: Find \( y \) from the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 1 \Rightarrow y = 4; \qquad x = 3 \Rightarrow y = 12
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute back into \( y = 4x \) --- the linear form is simpler and cannot create extra roots.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (1, 4)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (3, 12)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 06\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-006)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 06 (ise5-006)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = 2x - 1 \) and \( y = x^2 - 4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the two expressions for \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 4 = 2x - 1
\end{gathered}\]
Both sides equal \( y \), so they equal each other at any intersection point.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange to standard form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x - 3 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Move \( 2x - 1 \) to the left: \( -4 - (-1) = -3 \), \( -(2x) = -2x \).

\textit{Step 3: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 3)(x + 1) = 0 \implies x = 3 \text{ or } x = -1
\end{gathered}\]
Numbers multiplying to \( -3 \) and summing to \( -2 \): \( -3 \) and \( +1 \).

\textit{Step 4: Substitute into the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 3 \Rightarrow y = 5; \qquad x = -1 \Rightarrow y = -3
\end{gathered}\]
Pair each \( x \) with its own \( y \) --- a common pitfall is to mix them up.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (3, 5)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-1, -3)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 07\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-007)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 07 (ise5-007)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( x + y = 5 \) and \( y = x^2 - 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rearrange the linear equation to make \( y \) the subject.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 5 - x
\end{gathered}\]
Once \( y \) is the subject of the linear equation, you can substitute straight into the quadratic.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute into the quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
5 - x = x^2 - 1
\end{gathered}\]
Replace \( y \) in the second equation with \( 5 - x \) from the first.

\textit{Step 3: Rearrange to standard form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + x - 6 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Move every term to one side: \( -1 - 5 = -6 \), \( -(-x) = +x \).

\textit{Step 4: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x + 3)(x - 2) = 0 \implies x = -3 \text{ or } x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers multiplying to \( -6 \) and summing to \( 1 \): \( +3, -2 \).

\textit{Step 5: Find each \( y \) from the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = -3 \Rightarrow y = 8; \qquad x = 2 \Rightarrow y = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Always substitute back into \( y = 5 - x \) --- using the quadratic could introduce extraneous solutions.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-3, 8)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (2, 3)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\node[above left,scale=0.7] at (6.533,2.677) {$(2,3)$};
\end{tikzpicture}
}
\end{center}
\small The two equations plotted, with the solution shown at the point(s) of intersection.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 08\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-008)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 08 (ise5-008)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( 2x + y = 7 \) and \( y = x^2 + 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Make \( y \) the subject of the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 7 - 2x
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract \( 2x \) from both sides to isolate \( y \).

\textit{Step 2: Substitute into the quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
7 - 2x = x^2 + 2
\end{gathered}\]
This produces a single quadratic in \( x \).

\textit{Step 3: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 2x - 5 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Bring everything to the left-hand side: \( 2 - 7 = -5 \), \( +2x \).

\textit{Step 4: Apply the quadratic formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \dfrac{-2 \pm \sqrt{4 + 20}}{2} = \dfrac{-2 \pm \sqrt{24}}{2} = -1 \pm \sqrt{6}
\end{gathered}\]
Simplify \( \sqrt{24} = 2\sqrt{6} \) and cancel the factor of 2.

\textit{Step 5: Find the corresponding \( y \)-values.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = -1 + \sqrt{6} \Rightarrow y = 9 - 2\sqrt{6}; \qquad x = -1 - \sqrt{6} \Rightarrow y = 9 + 2\sqrt{6}
\end{gathered}\]
Use \( y = 7 - 2x \): \( 7 - 2(-1 \pm \sqrt{6}) = 9 \mp 2\sqrt{6} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-1 + \sqrt{6}, 9 - 2\sqrt{6})\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-1 - \sqrt{6}, 9 + 2\sqrt{6})\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 09\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-009)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 09 (ise5-009)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( x + y = 6 \) and \( y = x^2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rearrange the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 6 - x
\end{gathered}\]
Make \( y \) the subject so it can be substituted into the quadratic.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute.}
\[\begin{gathered}
6 - x = x^2
\end{gathered}\]
Replace \( y \) in \( y = x^2 \) with \( 6 - x \).

\textit{Step 3: Form a quadratic equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + x - 6 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Move everything to one side; the constant becomes \( -6 \) and the \( x \) term becomes \( +x \).

\textit{Step 4: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x + 3)(x - 2) = 0 \implies x = -3 \text{ or } x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers with product \( -6 \) and sum \( 1 \): \( +3 \) and \( -2 \).

\textit{Step 5: Find each \( y \) from \( y = 6 - x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = -3 \Rightarrow y = 9; \qquad x = 2 \Rightarrow y = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Always go back to the linear equation to keep the working safe and the pairing clear.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-3, 9)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (2, 4)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 10\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-010)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 10 (ise5-010)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( 3x + y = 1 \) and \( y = 2x^2 - x - 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Make \( y \) the subject of the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 1 - 3x
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract \( 3x \) from both sides.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute into the quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
1 - 3x = 2x^2 - x - 3
\end{gathered}\]
Replace \( y \) in the quadratic with the linear expression.

\textit{Step 3: Rearrange to standard form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x^2 + 2x - 4 = 0 \implies x^2 + x - 2 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Bring all terms to one side, then divide through by the common factor of 2 to simplify.

\textit{Step 4: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x + 2)(x - 1) = 0 \implies x = -2 \text{ or } x = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Numbers multiplying to \( -2 \) and summing to \( 1 \) are \( +2 \) and \( -1 \).

\textit{Step 5: Find each \( y \) from \( y = 1 - 3x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = -2 \Rightarrow y = 7; \qquad x = 1 \Rightarrow y = -2
\end{gathered}\]
Use the linear equation to avoid extraneous solutions.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-2, 7)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (1, -2)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 11\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-011)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 11 (ise5-011)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = x + 1 \) and \( x^2 + y^2 = 25 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: The first equation already has \( y \) as the subject.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x + 1
\end{gathered}\]
Geometrically, this is a straight line. The second equation is a circle centred at the origin with radius 5.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute into the circle.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + (x + 1)^2 = 25
\end{gathered}\]
Replace \( y \) with \( x + 1 \). Remember to put the bracket round it before squaring.

\textit{Step 3: Expand and simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + x^2 + 2x + 1 = 25 \implies 2x^2 + 2x - 24 = 0 \implies x^2 + x - 12 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Expand the bracket \( (x+1)^2 = x^2 + 2x + 1 \), combine like terms, then divide by 2.

\textit{Step 4: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x + 4)(x - 3) = 0 \implies x = -4 \text{ or } x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Numbers with product \( -12 \) and sum \( 1 \): \( +4 \) and \( -3 \).

\textit{Step 5: Find each \( y \) from the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = -4 \Rightarrow y = -3; \qquad x = 3 \Rightarrow y = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Quick sanity check: \( (-4)^2 + (-3)^2 = 16 + 9 = 25 \) and \( 3^2 + 4^2 = 9 + 16 = 25 \). Both lie on the circle.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-4, -3)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (3, 4)\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 12\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-012)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 12 (ise5-012)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = 2x \) and \( x^2 + y^2 = 20 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute \( y = 2x \) into the circle equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + (2x)^2 = 20
\end{gathered}\]
Square \( 2x \) to get \( 4x^2 \); make sure the whole \( 2x \) is squared, not just \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: Simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
5x^2 = 20 \implies x^2 = 4 \implies x = \pm 2
\end{gathered}\]
A simple quadratic with no linear term. Taking square roots gives two solutions.

\textit{Step 3: Find each \( y \) using \( y = 2x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2 \Rightarrow y = 4; \qquad x = -2 \Rightarrow y = -4
\end{gathered}\]
Pair each \( x \) with its own \( y \). The line through the origin produces symmetric points on the circle.

\textit{Step 4: Verify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2^2 + 4^2 = 4 + 16 = 20 \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
A quick check confirms both points lie on the circle.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (2, 4)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-2, -4)\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 13\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-013)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 13 (ise5-013)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( x + y = 7 \) and \( x^2 + y^2 = 25 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rearrange the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 7 - x
\end{gathered}\]
Make \( y \) the subject so you can substitute it into the circle.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + (7 - x)^2 = 25
\end{gathered}\]
Put the linear expression in brackets and square the whole thing.

\textit{Step 3: Expand and simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 49 - 14x + x^2 = 25 \implies 2x^2 - 14x + 24 = 0 \implies x^2 - 7x + 12 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Expand \( (7-x)^2 = 49 - 14x + x^2 \), gather terms, then divide by 2.

\textit{Step 4: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 3)(x - 4) = 0 \implies x = 3 \text{ or } x = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers with product 12 and sum \( -7 \): \( -3 \) and \( -4 \).

\textit{Step 5: Find each \( y \) from \( y = 7 - x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 3 \Rightarrow y = 4; \qquad x = 4 \Rightarrow y = 3
\end{gathered}\]
The two points are reflections of each other in the line \( y = x \), as expected from the symmetry of the system.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (3, 4)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (4, 3)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 14\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-014)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 14 (ise5-014)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = x - 1 \) and \( x^2 + y^2 = 13 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute the linear into the circle.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + (x - 1)^2 = 13
\end{gathered}\]
Bracket the linear expression before squaring to avoid sign errors.

\textit{Step 2: Expand and simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + x^2 - 2x + 1 = 13 \implies 2x^2 - 2x - 12 = 0 \implies x^2 - x - 6 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( (x-1)^2 = x^2 - 2x + 1 \). After dividing by 2 the numbers become friendlier.

\textit{Step 3: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 3)(x + 2) = 0 \implies x = 3 \text{ or } x = -2
\end{gathered}\]
Product \( -6 \), sum \( -1 \): \( -3 \) and \( +2 \).

\textit{Step 4: Find each \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 3 \Rightarrow y = 2; \qquad x = -2 \Rightarrow y = -3
\end{gathered}\]
Use \( y = x - 1 \). Check: \( 3^2 + 2^2 = 13 \checkmark \) and \( (-2)^2 + (-3)^2 = 13 \checkmark \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (3, 2)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-2, -3)\)
\end{answerbox}

\small The two equations plotted, with the solution shown at the point(s) of intersection.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 15\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-015)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 15 (ise5-015)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = x + 2 \) and \( y = (x - 1)(x + 3) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand the right-hand side of the quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 1)(x + 3) = x^2 + 2x - 3
\end{gathered}\]
Expanding first makes the algebra in the next step much cleaner.

\textit{Step 2: Equate the two expressions for \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x + 2 = x^2 + 2x - 3
\end{gathered}\]
Set the right-hand sides equal because both equal \( y \) at an intersection point.

\textit{Step 3: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
0 = x^2 + x - 5
\end{gathered}\]
\( 2x - x = x \) and \( -3 - 2 = -5 \).

\textit{Step 4: Apply the quadratic formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \dfrac{-1 \pm \sqrt{1 + 20}}{2} = \dfrac{-1 \pm \sqrt{21}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant \( 1 - 4(1)(-5) = 21 \), which is not a perfect square, so the answers are irrational.

\textit{Step 5: Find each corresponding \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x + 2 = \dfrac{3 \pm \sqrt{21}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Add 2 to each \( x \)-value: \( \tfrac{-1 \pm \sqrt{21}}{2} + 2 = \tfrac{3 \pm \sqrt{21}}{2} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = \left(\dfrac{-1 + \sqrt{21}}{2}, \dfrac{3 + \sqrt{21}}{2}\right)\) and \(\displaystyle \left(\dfrac{-1 - \sqrt{21}}{2}, \dfrac{3 - \sqrt{21}}{2}\right)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 16\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-016)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 16 (ise5-016)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = 2x + 1 \) and \( y = (x + 1)(x - 2) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand the bracketed quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x + 1)(x - 2) = x^2 - x - 2
\end{gathered}\]
Use FOIL: \( x \cdot x + x \cdot (-2) + 1 \cdot x + 1 \cdot (-2) = x^2 - x - 2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Set the two expressions for \( y \) equal.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x + 1 = x^2 - x - 2
\end{gathered}\]
Eliminate \( y \) by setting the right-hand sides equal.

\textit{Step 3: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
0 = x^2 - 3x - 3
\end{gathered}\]
\( -x - 2x = -3x \) and \( -2 - 1 = -3 \).

\textit{Step 4: Apply the quadratic formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \dfrac{3 \pm \sqrt{9 + 12}}{2} = \dfrac{3 \pm \sqrt{21}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant \( 9 - 4(1)(-3) = 21 \). Surd answers are perfectly acceptable.

\textit{Step 5: Find each corresponding \( y \) from \( y = 2x + 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 2 \cdot \dfrac{3 \pm \sqrt{21}}{2} + 1 = 3 \pm \sqrt{21} + 1 = 4 \pm \sqrt{21}
\end{gathered}\]
The factor of 2 cancels neatly with the denominator.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = \left(\dfrac{3 + \sqrt{21}}{2}, 4 + \sqrt{21}\right)\) and \(\displaystyle \left(\dfrac{3 - \sqrt{21}}{2}, 4 - \sqrt{21}\right)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 17\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-017)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 17 (ise5-017)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = 5 - x \) and \( y = (x - 1)(x - 2) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand the quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 1)(x - 2) = x^2 - 3x + 2
\end{gathered}\]
\( (-1)(-2) = +2 \) and \( -x - 2x = -3x \).

\textit{Step 2: Equate the two expressions for \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
5 - x = x^2 - 3x + 2
\end{gathered}\]
Both right-hand sides equal \( y \), so they must equal one another.

\textit{Step 3: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x - 3 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( -3x - (-x) = -2x \), \( 2 - 5 = -3 \).

\textit{Step 4: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 3)(x + 1) = 0 \implies x = 3 \text{ or } x = -1
\end{gathered}\]
Product \( -3 \), sum \( -2 \).

\textit{Step 5: Find each \( y \) from the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 3 \Rightarrow y = 2; \qquad x = -1 \Rightarrow y = 6
\end{gathered}\]
Use \( y = 5 - x \) --- the linear equation cannot create false solutions.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (3, 2)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-1, 6)\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 18\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-018)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 18 (ise5-018)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = 4 - x \) and \( y = (x + 2)(x - 1) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand the quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x + 2)(x - 1) = x^2 + x - 2
\end{gathered}\]
\( 2 \cdot (-1) = -2 \) and \( -x + 2x = x \).

\textit{Step 2: Set the right-hand sides equal.}
\[\begin{gathered}
4 - x = x^2 + x - 2
\end{gathered}\]
Equate both expressions for \( y \).

\textit{Step 3: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 2x - 6 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( x - (-x) = 2x \), \( -2 - 4 = -6 \).

\textit{Step 4: Apply the quadratic formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \dfrac{-2 \pm \sqrt{4 + 24}}{2} = \dfrac{-2 \pm \sqrt{28}}{2} = -1 \pm \sqrt{7}
\end{gathered}\]
Simplify \( \sqrt{28} = 2\sqrt{7} \) and cancel the 2.

\textit{Step 5: Find each \( y \) from \( y = 4 - x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 4 - (-1 \pm \sqrt{7}) = 5 \mp \sqrt{7}
\end{gathered}\]
The sign of the surd flips when subtracting.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-1 + \sqrt{7}, 5 - \sqrt{7})\) and \(\displaystyle (-1 - \sqrt{7}, 5 + \sqrt{7})\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 19\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-019)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 19 (ise5-019)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = x + 4 \) and \( y = x^2 + 2x - 1 \), giving your answers as exact surds.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the two expressions for \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x + 4 = x^2 + 2x - 1
\end{gathered}\]
Set the linear equal to the quadratic at any intersection point.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + x - 5 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( 2x - x = x \) and \( -1 - 4 = -5 \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply the quadratic formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \dfrac{-1 \pm \sqrt{1 + 20}}{2} = \dfrac{-1 \pm \sqrt{21}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant 21 is not a perfect square, so the roots are irrational. Leave them as surds rather than rounding.

\textit{Step 4: Find each \( y \) from \( y = x + 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \dfrac{-1 \pm \sqrt{21}}{2} + 4 = \dfrac{7 \pm \sqrt{21}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 4 = \dfrac{8}{2} \), so adding gives \( \dfrac{-1 + 8 \pm \sqrt{21}}{2} = \dfrac{7 \pm \sqrt{21}}{2} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = \left(\dfrac{-1 + \sqrt{21}}{2}, \dfrac{7 + \sqrt{21}}{2}\right)\) and \(\displaystyle \left(\dfrac{-1 - \sqrt{21}}{2}, \dfrac{7 - \sqrt{21}}{2}\right)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 20\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-020)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 20 (ise5-020)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = x - 2 \) and \( y = x^2 - 5 \), giving answers as exact surds.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the right-hand sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x - 2 = x^2 - 5
\end{gathered}\]
Set the two expressions for \( y \) equal at the points of intersection.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - x - 3 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Bring all terms to one side: \( -5 + 2 = -3 \), \( -x \) appears with a sign change.

\textit{Step 3: Quadratic formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \dfrac{1 \pm \sqrt{1 + 12}}{2} = \dfrac{1 \pm \sqrt{13}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant \( 1 + 12 = 13 \). Not a perfect square, so leave as a surd.

\textit{Step 4: Find each \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \dfrac{1 \pm \sqrt{13}}{2} - 2 = \dfrac{-3 \pm \sqrt{13}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
\( -2 = -\dfrac{4}{2} \), so \( \dfrac{1 - 4 \pm \sqrt{13}}{2} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = \left(\dfrac{1 + \sqrt{13}}{2}, \dfrac{-3 + \sqrt{13}}{2}\right)\) and \(\displaystyle \left(\dfrac{1 - \sqrt{13}}{2}, \dfrac{-3 - \sqrt{13}}{2}\right)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 21\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-021)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 21 (ise5-021)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = 2x \) and \( y = x^2 - 3 \), giving answers as exact surds.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the expressions for \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x = x^2 - 3
\end{gathered}\]
Substituting eliminates \( y \) and gives a quadratic in \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x - 3 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract \( 2x \) from both sides.

\textit{Step 3: This actually factorises.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 3)(x + 1) = 0 \implies x = 3 \text{ or } x = -1
\end{gathered}\]
Product \( -3 \), sum \( -2 \). Sometimes the "surd" version actually gives rational answers --- always check whether the discriminant is a perfect square first.

\textit{Step 4: Find each \( y \) from \( y = 2x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 3 \Rightarrow y = 6; \qquad x = -1 \Rightarrow y = -2
\end{gathered}\]
In this case the answers are integers, so no surds are needed in the final answer.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (3, 6)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-1, -2)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 22\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-022)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 22 (ise5-022)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = 3 - 2x \) and \( y = x^2 + x - 4 \), giving your answers as exact surds.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the right-hand sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3 - 2x = x^2 + x - 4
\end{gathered}\]
Both expressions equal \( y \), so they equal one another at any intersection.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 3x - 7 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( x - (-2x) = 3x \) and \( -4 - 3 = -7 \).

\textit{Step 3: Quadratic formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \dfrac{-3 \pm \sqrt{9 + 28}}{2} = \dfrac{-3 \pm \sqrt{37}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant \( 9 + 28 = 37 \), not a perfect square, so the roots are irrational.

\textit{Step 4: Find each \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 3 - 2 \cdot \dfrac{-3 \pm \sqrt{37}}{2} = 3 - (-3 \pm \sqrt{37}) = 6 \mp \sqrt{37}
\end{gathered}\]
The 2 in front cancels the denominator, then a sign flip occurs because of the minus.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = \left(\dfrac{-3 + \sqrt{37}}{2}, 6 - \sqrt{37}\right)\) and \(\displaystyle \left(\dfrac{-3 - \sqrt{37}}{2}, 6 + \sqrt{37}\right)\)
\end{answerbox}

\end{center}
\small The two equations plotted, with the solution shown at the point(s) of intersection.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 23\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-023)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 23 (ise5-023)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( y = 2x - 1 \) and \( y = x^2 \). Interpret your answer geometrically.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the expressions for \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 2x - 1
\end{gathered}\]
Setting the two right-hand sides equal eliminates \( y \).

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x + 1 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Move every term to the left.

\textit{Step 3: Compute the discriminant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
b^2 - 4ac = (-2)^2 - 4(1)(1) = 4 - 4 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
A discriminant of zero means the quadratic has exactly one (repeated) real root, so the line meets the curve at exactly one point.

\textit{Step 4: Solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 1)^2 = 0 \implies x = 1
\end{gathered}\]
The factorisation is a perfect square.

\textit{Step 5: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 2(1) - 1 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 1 \) into the linear equation.

\textit{Step 6: Geometric interpretation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Discriminant } = 0 \implies \text{line is tangent to the curve at } (1, 1).
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant zero is the algebraic signature of tangency: the line touches the curve at one point without crossing it.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (1, 1)\); the line is tangent to the parabola at this point.
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 24\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-024)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 24 (ise5-024)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Show that the line \( y = 4x - 4 \) is a tangent to the curve \( y = x^2 \) and find the point of tangency.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the right-hand sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 4x - 4
\end{gathered}\]
A tangent meets the curve at exactly one (repeated) point --- we will check this via the discriminant.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange to standard form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 4x + 4 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Move everything to one side.

\textit{Step 3: Compute the discriminant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
b^2 - 4ac = 16 - 16 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The discriminant is zero --- confirming the line is tangent to the curve.

\textit{Step 4: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 2)^2 = 0 \implies x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
A perfect-square factorisation.

\textit{Step 5: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 4(2) - 4 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute back into the linear equation.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Tangent at \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (2, 4)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 25\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-025)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 25 (ise5-025)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Show that the line \( y = -2x + 1 \) is tangent to the curve \( y = -x^2 \), and find the point of contact.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set the right-hand sides equal.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-x^2 = -2x + 1
\end{gathered}\]
Both equal \( y \) at any point of contact, so the right-hand sides must agree there.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange to standard form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x + 1 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply both sides by \( -1 \) (which reverses every sign) and bring all terms to the left so the leading coefficient is positive.

\textit{Step 3: Compute the discriminant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
b^2 - 4ac = (-2)^2 - 4(1)(1) = 4 - 4 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
A discriminant of zero is the algebraic signature of tangency --- exactly one (repeated) real root, so the line touches the curve at one point.

\textit{Step 4: Solve the (perfect-square) quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 1)^2 = 0 \implies x = 1
\end{gathered}\]
The factorisation is a perfect square, giving the repeated root \( x = 1 \).

\textit{Step 5: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -2(1) + 1 = -1
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 1 \) into the linear equation. Check on the curve: \( -1^2 = -1 \checkmark \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad The line is tangent to the curve at \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (1, -1)\).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 26\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-026)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 26 (ise5-026)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Show that the line \( y = 6x - 9 \) is tangent to the curve \( y = x^2 \), and find the point of tangency.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the two expressions for \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 6x - 9
\end{gathered}\]
Set the linear right-hand side equal to the quadratic right-hand side.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 6x + 9 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
A standard form quadratic.

\textit{Step 3: Compute the discriminant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
b^2 - 4ac = 36 - 36 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant zero confirms tangency --- exactly one (repeated) root.

\textit{Step 4: Solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 3)^2 = 0 \implies x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
A perfect-square trinomial.

\textit{Step 5: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 6(3) - 9 = 9
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 3 \) into the linear equation.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad The line is tangent at \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (3, 9)\).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 27\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-027)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 27 (ise5-027)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Show that the line \( y = x - 3 \) does not meet the curve \( y = x^2 + 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the two expressions for \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x - 3 = x^2 + 1
\end{gathered}\]
Eliminate \( y \) by setting the right-hand sides equal.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - x + 4 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( 1 + 3 = 4 \), and moving \( x \) across gives \( -x \).

\textit{Step 3: Compute the discriminant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
b^2 - 4ac = 1 - 16 = -15 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
A negative discriminant means there are no real solutions, so the line and the curve do not intersect.

\textit{Step 4: Geometric interpretation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Line misses the curve entirely.}
\end{gathered}\]
The line passes "under" the parabola; the parabola has minimum value 1 (at \( x = 0 \)), but the line at \( x = 0 \) gives \( y = -3 \), and the line never rises fast enough to catch the parabola.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad No real solutions --- discriminant \(\displaystyle = -15 < 0\), so the line does not meet the curve.
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 28\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-028)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 28 (ise5-028)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Show that the line \( y = 2x + 5 \) does not intersect the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute the line into the circle equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + (2x + 5)^2 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Replace \( y \) in the circle equation with the linear expression so the system reduces to a single quadratic in \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: Expand and simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 4x^2 + 20x + 25 = 1 \implies 5x^2 + 20x + 24 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Expand \( (2x+5)^2 = 4x^2 + 20x + 25 \), then bring the \( 1 \) across to the left-hand side.

\textit{Step 3: Compute the discriminant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
b^2 - 4ac = 20^2 - 4(5)(24) = 400 - 480 = -80
\end{gathered}\]
A negative discriminant means the quadratic has no real solutions, so there is no real \( x \) at which the line meets the circle.

\textit{Step 4: Geometric interpretation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{discriminant } < 0 \implies \text{no intersection.}
\end{gathered}\]
The perpendicular distance from the origin to the line \( 2x - y + 5 = 0 \) is \( \tfrac{5}{\sqrt{5}} = \sqrt{5} > 1 \), which exceeds the radius --- confirming the line lies entirely outside the unit circle.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad No real intersection --- discriminant \(\displaystyle = -80 < 0\), so the line does not meet the circle.
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 29\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-029)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 29 (ise5-029)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Show that the line \( y = -x - 5 \) does not meet the curve \( y = x^2 - 3x + 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the expressions for \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
-x - 5 = x^2 - 3x + 2
\end{gathered}\]
Set the two right-hand sides equal.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x + 7 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( -3x - (-x) = -2x \), \( 2 - (-5) = 7 \).

\textit{Step 3: Compute the discriminant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
b^2 - 4ac = 4 - 28 = -24 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
Negative discriminant --- there are no real roots, so the line and curve do not meet.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad No real intersection; discriminant \(\displaystyle = -24 < 0\).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 30\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-030)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 30 (ise5-030)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Show that the line \( y = 3x + 4 \) does not intersect the curve \( y = x^2 + x + 6 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the right-hand sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x + 4 = x^2 + x + 6
\end{gathered}\]
Eliminate \( y \) by setting the two expressions for \( y \) equal at any potential intersection.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange to standard form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x + 2 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Move everything to one side: \( x - 3x = -2x \) and \( 6 - 4 = 2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Compute the discriminant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
b^2 - 4ac = (-2)^2 - 4(1)(2) = 4 - 8 = -4
\end{gathered}\]
A negative discriminant means the quadratic has no real roots, so there is no real \( x \) at which the line meets the curve.

\textit{Step 4: Geometric interpretation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{discriminant } < 0 \implies \text{line misses the curve entirely.}
\end{gathered}\]
Completing the square, \( x^2 + x + 6 - (3x+4) = (x-1)^2 + 1 \geq 1 > 0 \) for every real \( x \), so the curve sits strictly above the line.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad No real intersection --- discriminant \(\displaystyle = -4 < 0\), so the line does not meet the curve.
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 31\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-031)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 31 (ise5-031)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( x + y = 5 \) and \( xy = 6 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rearrange the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 5 - x
\end{gathered}\]
Make \( y \) the subject of the simpler equation.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute into \( xy = 6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x(5 - x) = 6 \implies 5x - x^2 = 6
\end{gathered}\]
Distribute, then bring everything to one side.

\textit{Step 3: Rearrange and factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 5x + 6 = 0 \implies (x - 2)(x - 3) = 0 \implies x = 2 \text{ or } x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers with product 6 and sum \( -5 \): \( -2, -3 \).

\textit{Step 4: Find each \( y \) from \( y = 5 - x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2 \Rightarrow y = 3; \qquad x = 3 \Rightarrow y = 2
\end{gathered}\]
The system is symmetric in \( x \) and \( y \), so swapping their roles gives the other solution.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (2, 3)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (3, 2)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 32\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-032)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 32 (ise5-032)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( 2x + y = 7 \) and \( xy = 6 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rearrange the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 7 - 2x
\end{gathered}\]
Make \( y \) the subject.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute into \( xy = 6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x(7 - 2x) = 6 \implies 7x - 2x^2 = 6
\end{gathered}\]
Distribute over the bracket.

\textit{Step 3: Rearrange and factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x^2 - 7x + 6 = 0 \implies (2x - 3)(x - 2) = 0 \implies x = \dfrac{3}{2} \text{ or } x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply: \( 2x \cdot x = 2x^2 \), \( 2x \cdot (-2) + (-3) \cdot x = -4x - 3x = -7x \), \( (-3)(-2) = 6 \). Check.

\textit{Step 4: Find each \( y \) from \( y = 7 - 2x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \dfrac{3}{2} \Rightarrow y = 4; \qquad x = 2 \Rightarrow y = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Quick check: \( \tfrac{3}{2} \times 4 = 6 \checkmark \) and \( 2 \times 3 = 6 \checkmark \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = \left(\dfrac{3}{2}, 4\right)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (2, 3)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 33\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-033)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 33 (ise5-033)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( x - y = 1 \) and \( xy = 12 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = y + 1
\end{gathered}\]
Making \( x \) the subject is just as valid as making \( y \) the subject.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute into \( xy = 12 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(y + 1)y = 12 \implies y^2 + y - 12 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Expand and bring everything to one side.

\textit{Step 3: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(y + 4)(y - 3) = 0 \implies y = -4 \text{ or } y = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers multiplying to \( -12 \) and summing to \( 1 \): \( +4 \) and \( -3 \).

\textit{Step 4: Find each \( x \) from \( x = y + 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -4 \Rightarrow x = -3; \qquad y = 3 \Rightarrow x = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Sanity check the product: \( (-3)(-4) = 12 \checkmark \) and \( 4 \times 3 = 12 \checkmark \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-3, -4)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (4, 3)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 34\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-034)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 34 (ise5-034)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Solve the simultaneous equations \( 3x + 2y = 12 \) and \( xy = 6 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rearrange the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \dfrac{12 - 3x}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Make \( y \) the subject.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute into \( xy = 6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \cdot \dfrac{12 - 3x}{2} = 6 \implies x(12 - 3x) = 12
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply both sides by 2 to clear the fraction.

\textit{Step 3: Expand and rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
12x - 3x^2 = 12 \implies 3x^2 - 12x + 12 = 0 \implies x^2 - 4x + 4 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Divide through by 3 for cleaner numbers.

\textit{Step 4: Solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 2)^2 = 0 \implies x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
A perfect square --- there is exactly one repeated solution, meaning the line is tangent to the rectangular hyperbola.

\textit{Step 5: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \dfrac{12 - 6}{2} = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 2 \) into the rearranged linear.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (2, 3)\); the line is tangent to the hyperbola \(\displaystyle xy = 6\) at this point.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 35\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-035)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 35 (ise5-035)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Find the points of intersection of the line \( y = 2x - 1 \) with the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 25 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute \( y = 2x - 1 \) into the circle.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + (2x - 1)^2 = 25
\end{gathered}\]
Bracket the linear expression before squaring to avoid sign errors.

\textit{Step 2: Expand.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 4x^2 - 4x + 1 = 25 \implies 5x^2 - 4x - 24 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( (2x-1)^2 = 4x^2 - 4x + 1 \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve the quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \dfrac{4 \pm \sqrt{16 + 480}}{10} = \dfrac{4 \pm \sqrt{496}}{10}
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant \( 16 + 480 = 496 = 16 \times 31 \), so \( \sqrt{496} = 4\sqrt{31} \).

\textit{Step 4: Simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \dfrac{4 \pm 4\sqrt{31}}{10} = \dfrac{2 \pm 2\sqrt{31}}{5}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide numerator and denominator by 2.

\textit{Step 5: Find each \( y \) from \( y = 2x - 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 2 \cdot \dfrac{2 \pm 2\sqrt{31}}{5} - 1 = \dfrac{4 \pm 4\sqrt{31}}{5} - \dfrac{5}{5} = \dfrac{-1 \pm 4\sqrt{31}}{5}
\end{gathered}\]
Combine over a common denominator.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = \left(\dfrac{2 + 2\sqrt{31}}{5}, \dfrac{-1 + 4\sqrt{31}}{5}\right)\) and \(\displaystyle \left(\dfrac{2 - 2\sqrt{31}}{5}, \dfrac{-1 - 4\sqrt{31}}{5}\right)\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 36\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-036)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 36 (ise5-036)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Find the points where the line \( x + 2y = 5 \) meets the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 10 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rearrange the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 5 - 2y
\end{gathered}\]
Make \( x \) the subject.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute into the circle.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(5 - 2y)^2 + y^2 = 10
\end{gathered}\]
Replace \( x \) with \( 5 - 2y \) in the circle equation.

\textit{Step 3: Expand and simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
25 - 20y + 4y^2 + y^2 = 10 \implies 5y^2 - 20y + 15 = 0 \implies y^2 - 4y + 3 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Divide through by 5 for friendlier numbers.

\textit{Step 4: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(y - 1)(y - 3) = 0 \implies y = 1 \text{ or } y = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Product 3, sum \( -4 \): \( -1, -3 \).

\textit{Step 5: Find each \( x \) from \( x = 5 - 2y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 1 \Rightarrow x = 3; \qquad y = 3 \Rightarrow x = -1
\end{gathered}\]
Sanity check: \( 3^2 + 1^2 = 10 \checkmark \), \( (-1)^2 + 3^2 = 10 \checkmark \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (3, 1)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-1, 3)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 37\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-037)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 37 (ise5-037)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Find the intersection points of the line \( y = x \) with the circle \( (x - 2)^2 + (y - 1)^2 = 5 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute \( y = x \) into the circle.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 2)^2 + (x - 1)^2 = 5
\end{gathered}\]
Both \( x \) and \( y \) appear in the brackets --- replacing \( y \) with \( x \) is straightforward.

\textit{Step 2: Expand.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 4x + 4 + x^2 - 2x + 1 = 5 \implies 2x^2 - 6x = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Combine like terms and move the constant to the left.

\textit{Step 3: Factor and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x(x - 3) = 0 \implies x = 0 \text{ or } x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Factor out \( 2x \) from both terms.

\textit{Step 4: Find each \( y \) using \( y = x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \Rightarrow y = 0; \qquad x = 3 \Rightarrow y = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Verify: \( (0-2)^2 + (0-1)^2 = 4 + 1 = 5 \checkmark \) and \( (3-2)^2 + (3-1)^2 = 1 + 4 = 5 \checkmark \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (0, 0)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (3, 3)\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 38\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-038)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 38 (ise5-038)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Find the points of intersection of the line \( y = -x + 4 \) with the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 8 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute the line into the circle.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + (-x + 4)^2 = 8
\end{gathered}\]
Replace \( y \) with \( -x + 4 \) and square the whole thing.

\textit{Step 2: Expand.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + x^2 - 8x + 16 = 8 \implies 2x^2 - 8x + 8 = 0 \implies x^2 - 4x + 4 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( (-x+4)^2 = x^2 - 8x + 16 \). Divide by 2 to simplify.

\textit{Step 3: Solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 2)^2 = 0 \implies x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
A repeated root --- the line is tangent to the circle.

\textit{Step 4: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -2 + 4 = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute into the linear equation.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad The line is tangent to the circle at \(\displaystyle (2, 2)\).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 39\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-039)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 39 (ise5-039)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{Find the value of \( k \) such that the line \( y = 2x + k \) is tangent to the curve \( y = x^2 \). State the point of contact.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set up the simultaneous equations and equate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 2x + k \implies x^2 - 2x - k = 0
\end{gathered}\]
For a tangent, the resulting quadratic in \( x \) must have a single (repeated) root --- i.e. discriminant zero.

\textit{Step 2: Set the discriminant to zero.}
\[\begin{gathered}
b^2 - 4ac = 4 + 4k = 0 \implies k = -1
\end{gathered}\]
Tangency is the algebraic statement "discriminant = 0".

\textit{Step 3: Find the tangent point by solving the (now perfect-square) quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x + 1 = 0 \implies (x - 1)^2 = 0 \implies x = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( k = -1 \) back into the quadratic. The repeated root is the \( x \)-coordinate of the tangent point.

\textit{Step 4: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 2(1) + (-1) = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 1 \) into the linear (with \( k = -1 \)).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle k = -1\); the tangent point is \(\displaystyle (1, 1)\).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 40\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-040)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 40 (ise5-040)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{Find the values of \( k \) for which the line \( y = x + k \) is tangent to the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 8 \). State each point of contact.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute the line into the circle.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + (x + k)^2 = 8
\end{gathered}\]
Replace \( y \) with \( x + k \) in the circle.

\textit{Step 2: Expand and simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + x^2 + 2kx + k^2 = 8 \implies 2x^2 + 2kx + (k^2 - 8) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
A quadratic in \( x \) with coefficients depending on \( k \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply the tangency condition: discriminant zero.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(2k)^2 - 4(2)(k^2 - 8) = 0 \implies 4k^2 - 8k^2 + 64 = 0 \implies -4k^2 = -64 \implies k = \pm 4
\end{gathered}\]
Two tangent lines exist --- one above the circle and one below --- corresponding to the two values of \( k \).

\textit{Step 4: Find the tangent points. For \( k = 4 \):}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x^2 + 8x + 8 = 0 \implies x^2 + 4x + 4 = 0 \implies (x + 2)^2 = 0 \implies x = -2, \; y = 2
\end{gathered}\]
A perfect-square trinomial with repeated root \( x = -2 \), giving the tangent point \( (-2, 2) \).

\textit{Step 5: For \( k = -4 \):}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x^2 - 8x + 8 = 0 \implies (x - 2)^2 = 0 \implies x = 2, \; y = -2
\end{gathered}\]
Tangent point \( (2, -2) \). The two tangent points are diametrically opposite on the circle.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle k = 4\); tangent at \(\displaystyle (-2, 2)\). \(\displaystyle k = -4\); tangent at \(\displaystyle (2, -2)\).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 41\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-041)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 41 (ise5-041)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{The line \( y = mx + 1 \) is tangent to the curve \( y = x^2 + 2 \). Find the possible values of \( m \) and the corresponding points of contact.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the two expressions.}
\[\begin{gathered}
mx + 1 = x^2 + 2 \implies x^2 - mx + 1 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Set the right-hand sides equal and rearrange into the standard quadratic.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the tangency condition.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(-m)^2 - 4(1)(1) = 0 \implies m^2 = 4 \implies m = \pm 2
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant zero gives two valid gradients --- there are exactly two lines through \( (0, 1) \) tangent to the parabola.

\textit{Step 3: For \( m = 2 \), find the tangent point.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x + 1 = 0 \implies (x - 1)^2 = 0 \implies x = 1, \; y = 2(1) + 1 = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute and solve the perfect-square equation; tangent point \( (1, 3) \).

\textit{Step 4: For \( m = -2 \):}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 2x + 1 = 0 \implies (x + 1)^2 = 0 \implies x = -1, \; y = -2(-1) + 1 = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Tangent point \( (-1, 3) \). By symmetry of \( y = x^2 + 2 \) about the \( y \)-axis, the two tangent points have the same \( y \)-coordinate.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle m = 2\); tangent at \(\displaystyle (1, 3)\). \(\displaystyle m = -2\); tangent at \(\displaystyle (-1, 3)\).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 42\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-042)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 42 (ise5-042)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Find the range of values of \( k \) for which the line \( y = 2x + k \) meets the curve \( y = x^2 + 1 \) at two distinct points.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate and rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x + k = x^2 + 1 \implies x^2 - 2x + (1 - k) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
A quadratic in \( x \) whose coefficients depend on \( k \).

\textit{Step 2: For two distinct intersection points, demand discriminant $>$ 0.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(-2)^2 - 4(1)(1 - k) > 0 \implies 4 - 4 + 4k > 0 \implies 4k > 0 \implies k > 0
\end{gathered}\]
Two distinct real roots correspond to two distinct intersection points. Discriminant $>$ 0 is exactly this condition.

\textit{Step 3: Interpret.}
\[\begin{gathered}
k > 0
\end{gathered}\]
When \( k = 0 \) the line is tangent (one intersection); when \( k < 0 \) it misses the curve; when \( k > 0 \) it cuts twice.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle k > 0\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 43\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-043)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 43 (ise5-043)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the points of intersection of the curves \( y = x^2 \) and \( y = 4 - x^2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set the two expressions for \( y \) equal.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 4 - x^2 \implies 2x^2 = 4 \implies x^2 = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Even though both equations are quadratic, the standard technique still works: equate the right-hand sides.

\textit{Step 2: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \pm \sqrt{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Take square roots, remembering both the positive and negative.

\textit{Step 3: Find each \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x^2 = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Either curve will give the same value of \( y \) for each \( x \) --- that is the whole point of an intersection.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (\sqrt{2}, 2)\) and \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (-\sqrt{2}, 2)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 44\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-044)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 44 (ise5-044)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Find the points of intersection of the parabolas \( y = x^2 - 2x \) and \( y = -x^2 + 4x - 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the right-hand sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x = -x^2 + 4x - 2
\end{gathered}\]
Set the two expressions for \( y \) equal.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x^2 - 6x + 2 = 0 \implies x^2 - 3x + 1 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Add \( x^2 \) and subtract \( 4x - 2 \); then divide through by 2.

\textit{Step 3: Apply the quadratic formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \dfrac{3 \pm \sqrt{9 - 4}}{2} = \dfrac{3 \pm \sqrt{5}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant 5 is not a perfect square, so the roots are irrational.

\textit{Step 4: Find each \( y \) --- use the simpler curve \( y = x^2 - 2x = x(x - 2) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \dfrac{3 + \sqrt{5}}{2} \Rightarrow x - 2 = \dfrac{-1 + \sqrt{5}}{2}, \; y = \dfrac{(3 + \sqrt{5})(-1 + \sqrt{5})}{4} = \dfrac{-3 + 3\sqrt{5} - \sqrt{5} + 5}{4} = \dfrac{2 + 2\sqrt{5}}{4} = \dfrac{1 + \sqrt{5}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Expand the product carefully, then simplify.

\textit{Step 5: Similarly for the other root.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \dfrac{3 - \sqrt{5}}{2} \Rightarrow y = \dfrac{1 - \sqrt{5}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Replace \( \sqrt{5} \) by \( -\sqrt{5} \) throughout the previous computation.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = \left(\dfrac{3 + \sqrt{5}}{2}, \dfrac{1 + \sqrt{5}}{2}\right)\) and \(\displaystyle \left(\dfrac{3 - \sqrt{5}}{2}, \dfrac{1 - \sqrt{5}}{2}\right)\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 45\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-045)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 45 (ise5-045)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{Find the points where the parabola \( y = x^2 - 3 \) meets the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 9 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute \( y = x^2 - 3 \) into the circle.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + (x^2 - 3)^2 = 9
\end{gathered}\]
Replace \( y \) in the circle equation. This is genuinely a quartic in \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: Expand.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + x^4 - 6x^2 + 9 = 9 \implies x^4 - 5x^2 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Expand \( (x^2 - 3)^2 = x^4 - 6x^2 + 9 \) and cancel the constants.

\textit{Step 3: Factor.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2(x^2 - 5) = 0 \implies x = 0 \text{ or } x = \pm \sqrt{5}
\end{gathered}\]
Treat as a quadratic in \( x^2 \) --- or factor out \( x^2 \) directly. Three distinct \( x \)-values (with \( x = 0 \) repeated).

\textit{Step 4: Find each \( y \) using \( y = x^2 - 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \Rightarrow y = -3; \qquad x = \pm \sqrt{5} \Rightarrow y = 5 - 3 = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Now we have three intersection points: \( (0, -3) \), \( (\sqrt{5}, 2) \), \( (-\sqrt{5}, 2) \).

\textit{Step 5: Verify on the circle.}
\[\begin{gathered}
0^2 + (-3)^2 = 9 \checkmark; \qquad 5 + 4 = 9 \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
All three points lie on the circle. The parabola is tangent to the circle at the bottom point and crosses it twice further up.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle (x, y) = (0, -3)\), \(\displaystyle (\sqrt{5}, 2)\) and \(\displaystyle (-\sqrt{5}, 2)\)
\end{answerbox}

\node[blue!70!black,scale=0.7,anchor=west,inner sep=1pt] at (1.317,9.233) {$y=x^2-3$};
\fill[red!75!black] (4.2,1.05) circle (2.2pt);
\node[below right,scale=0.7] at (4.2,1.05) {$(0,-3)$};

}
\end{center}
\small The two equations plotted, with the solution shown at the point(s) of intersection.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 46\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-046)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 46 (ise5-046)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{A rectangle has perimeter 22 cm and area 28 cm\(^2\). Find the lengths of its sides.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let \( x \) and \( y \) denote the side lengths and write down the two given conditions.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2(x + y) = 22 \implies x + y = 11; \qquad xy = 28
\end{gathered}\]
A rectangle of sides \( x \) and \( y \) has perimeter \( 2(x+y) \) and area \( xy \).

\textit{Step 2: Make \( y \) the subject of the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 11 - x
\end{gathered}\]
This lets you substitute into the area equation.

\textit{Step 3: Substitute and form a quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x(11 - x) = 28 \implies 11x - x^2 = 28 \implies x^2 - 11x + 28 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Move everything to one side so the leading coefficient is positive.

\textit{Step 4: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 4)(x - 7) = 0 \implies x = 4 \text{ or } x = 7
\end{gathered}\]
Product 28, sum \( -11 \): \( -4, -7 \).

\textit{Step 5: Find the partner length.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 4 \Rightarrow y = 7; \qquad x = 7 \Rightarrow y = 4
\end{gathered}\]
The two solutions correspond to the same rectangle with the role of "length" and "width" swapped.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad The sides are \(\displaystyle 4\) cm and \(\displaystyle 7\) cm.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 47\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-047)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 47 (ise5-047)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{The sum of two positive numbers is 12 and the sum of their squares is 80. Find the two numbers.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let the numbers be \( x \) and \( y \), and translate the conditions.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x + y = 12; \qquad x^2 + y^2 = 80
\end{gathered}\]
One linear, one quadratic --- classic structure for substitution.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 12 - x
\end{gathered}\]
Make \( y \) the subject so it can be substituted into the quadratic.

\textit{Step 3: Substitute.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + (12 - x)^2 = 80
\end{gathered}\]
Bracket the linear expression before squaring.

\textit{Step 4: Expand and simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 144 - 24x + x^2 = 80 \implies 2x^2 - 24x + 64 = 0 \implies x^2 - 12x + 32 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Divide through by 2 to simplify.

\textit{Step 5: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 4)(x - 8) = 0 \implies x = 4 \text{ or } x = 8
\end{gathered}\]
Product 32, sum \( -12 \): \( -4 \) and \( -8 \).

\textit{Step 6: Find each partner number.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 4 \Rightarrow y = 8; \qquad x = 8 \Rightarrow y = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Both solutions describe the same unordered pair of numbers.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad The two numbers are \(\displaystyle 4\) and \(\displaystyle 8\).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 48\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-048)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 48 (ise5-048)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{A right-angled triangle has hypotenuse 13 cm and perimeter 30 cm. Find the lengths of the two shorter sides.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let the two shorter sides be \( x \) and \( y \). Translate the conditions.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x + y + 13 = 30 \implies x + y = 17; \qquad x^2 + y^2 = 13^2 = 169
\end{gathered}\]
The perimeter equation is linear and Pythagoras gives the quadratic constraint.

\textit{Step 2: Make \( y \) the subject of the linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 17 - x
\end{gathered}\]
Ready for substitution.

\textit{Step 3: Substitute into Pythagoras.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + (17 - x)^2 = 169
\end{gathered}\]
Bracket before squaring.

\textit{Step 4: Expand and simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 289 - 34x + x^2 = 169 \implies 2x^2 - 34x + 120 = 0 \implies x^2 - 17x + 60 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Divide through by 2.

\textit{Step 5: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 5)(x - 12) = 0 \implies x = 5 \text{ or } x = 12
\end{gathered}\]
Product 60, sum \( -17 \): \( -5 \) and \( -12 \).

\textit{Step 6: Find the partner side and check.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 5 \Rightarrow y = 12; \qquad 5^2 + 12^2 = 25 + 144 = 169 \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
This is the classic 5-12-13 Pythagorean triple. Both solutions describe the same triangle, just with the labels of the legs swapped.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad The two shorter sides are \(\displaystyle 5\) cm and \(\displaystyle 12\) cm.
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 49\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-049)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 49 (ise5-049)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{The line \( y = x + 2 \) meets the curve \( y = x^2 - 4x + 8 \) at points \( A \) and \( B \). Find the coordinates of \( A \) and \( B \), and hence the length \( AB \) in exact form.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Equate the two expressions for \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x + 2 = x^2 - 4x + 8
\end{gathered}\]
Set the right-hand sides equal at the intersection points.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 5x + 6 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( -4x - x = -5x \), \( 8 - 2 = 6 \).

\textit{Step 3: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 2)(x - 3) = 0 \implies x = 2 \text{ or } x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Product 6, sum \( -5 \).

\textit{Step 4: Find each \( y \) and so the coordinates.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2 \Rightarrow y = 4; \qquad x = 3 \Rightarrow y = 5
\end{gathered}\]
So \( A = (2, 4) \) and \( B = (3, 5) \) (the labelling is arbitrary).

\textit{Step 5: Compute \( AB \) using the distance formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
AB = \sqrt{(3 - 2)^2 + (5 - 4)^2} = \sqrt{1 + 1} = \sqrt{2}
\end{gathered}\]
\( AB^2 = (\Delta x)^2 + (\Delta y)^2 \). Leave the answer in exact surd form unless asked otherwise.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle A = (2, 4)\), \(\displaystyle B = (3, 5)\); \(\displaystyle AB = \sqrt{2}\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 50\quad\small\texttt{(ise5-050)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Simultaneous Equations (Quadratic) 50 (ise5-050)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 8}

\question{The line \( y = 2x + 1 \) meets the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 10 \) at points \( P \) and \( Q \). Find the coordinates of \( P \) and \( Q \), the midpoint \( M \) of \( PQ \), and the length \( PQ \) in exact form.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute the line into the circle.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + (2x + 1)^2 = 10
\end{gathered}\]
Replace \( y \) with \( 2x + 1 \).

\textit{Step 2: Expand and simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 4x^2 + 4x + 1 = 10 \implies 5x^2 + 4x - 9 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( (2x+1)^2 = 4x^2 + 4x + 1 \), then move everything to one side.

\textit{Step 3: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(5x + 9)(x - 1) = 0 \implies x = -\dfrac{9}{5} \text{ or } x = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Check the expansion: \( 5x \cdot x = 5x^2 \), \( 5x \cdot (-1) + 9 \cdot x = -5x + 9x = 4x \), \( 9 \cdot (-1) = -9 \). Good.

\textit{Step 4: Find each \( y \) from \( y = 2x + 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 1 \Rightarrow y = 3; \qquad x = -\dfrac{9}{5} \Rightarrow y = -\dfrac{13}{5}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 2 \cdot (-\tfrac{9}{5}) + 1 = -\tfrac{18}{5} + \tfrac{5}{5} = -\tfrac{13}{5} \).

\textit{Step 5: Compute the midpoint \( M \) of \( PQ \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
M = \left( \dfrac{1 + (-\tfrac{9}{5})}{2}, \dfrac{3 + (-\tfrac{13}{5})}{2} \right) = \left( \dfrac{-\tfrac{4}{5}}{2}, \dfrac{\tfrac{2}{5}}{2} \right) = \left( -\dfrac{2}{5}, \dfrac{1}{5} \right)
\end{gathered}\]
Midpoint is the average of the coordinates. Combine over common denominators first.

\textit{Step 6: Compute \( PQ \) using the distance formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
PQ = \sqrt{\left(1 + \tfrac{9}{5}\right)^2 + \left(3 + \tfrac{13}{5}\right)^2} = \sqrt{\left(\tfrac{14}{5}\right)^2 + \left(\tfrac{28}{5}\right)^2} = \sqrt{\dfrac{196 + 784}{25}} = \sqrt{\dfrac{980}{25}} = \dfrac{\sqrt{980}}{5} = \dfrac{14\sqrt{5}}{5}
\end{gathered}\]
Note \( \tfrac{28}{5} = 2 \cdot \tfrac{14}{5} \), so the second component is twice the first --- consistent with a line of gradient 2. Simplify \( \sqrt{980} = \sqrt{196 \cdot 5} = 14\sqrt{5} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle P = (1, 3)\), \(\displaystyle Q = \left(-\dfrac{9}{5}, -\dfrac{13}{5}\right)\); midpoint \(\displaystyle M = \left(-\dfrac{2}{5}, \dfrac{1}{5}\right)\); \(\displaystyle PQ = \dfrac{14\sqrt{5}}{5}\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}
